========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I came in because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore. It’s gotten to the point where I sit at my kitchen table and I feel almost stuck. The table is just covered—my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, little half-finished lists I’ve started and then abandoned—and I keep telling myself, “All right, just pick one thing and start,” but I can’t seem to figure out what should come first.

And then I’ll sit there sorting a paper, moving it to another pile, reading the same note again, and suddenly the whole day has gone by and nothing is really done. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because these are ordinary things, not complicated things, but I feel like I can’t get them in any kind of order.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it’s not completely new. I mean, it’s gotten much harder to manage now, but when I think back, there were signs even when I was a little girl.

I remember my teachers would write things on my report cards like, “She’s bright, but careless,” which used to sting because I wasn’t trying to be careless. I’d forget to bring the right books home, or I’d leave homework somewhere, or I’d have it half done and not turned in. And they’d have to remind me to finish my seatwork before I could go outside to play. I can still picture sitting there, everybody else already lining up or going out, and I’d be looking down at my paper like, “Oh… I didn’t finish that.”

So, no, I wouldn’t say this came out of nowhere recently. It’s more like it’s been part of me for a very long time, but now it’s causing more trouble.

I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve been sitting around crying all day, every day, or feeling hopeless in that heavy way you mean, I don’t think. The table is just covered—my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, little half-finished lists I’ve started and then abandoned—and I keep telling myself, “All right, just pick one thing and start,” but I can’t seem to figure out what should come first.\n\nAnd then I’ll sit there sorting a paper, moving it to another pile, reading the same note again, and suddenly the whole day has gone by and nothing is really done. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because these are ordinary things, not complicated things, but I feel like I can’t get them in any kind of order.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of trouble organizing and starting tasks been something you've dealt with your whole life, or is this a new change for you recently?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not completely new. I mean, it’s gotten much harder to manage now, but when I think back, there were signs even when I was a little girl.\n\nI remember my teachers would write things on my report cards like, “She’s bright, but careless,” which used to sting because I wasn’t trying to be careless. I’d forget to bring the right books home, or I’d leave homework somewhere, or I’d have it half done and not turned in. And they’d have to remind me to finish my seatwork before I could go outside to play. I can still picture sitting there, everybody else already lining up or going out, and I’d be looking down at my paper like, “Oh… I didn’t finish that.”\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say this came out of nowhere recently. It’s more like it’s been part of me for a very long time, but now it’s causing more trouble.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you been feeling down, sad, or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve been sitting around crying all day, every day, or feeling hopeless in that heavy way you mean, I don’t think. I do get discouraged—very discouraged—when I look around and realize I still haven’t done the things I meant to do, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as being “down” most of the day.\n\nSome days I feel sort of worn out by myself, if that makes sense, like I’m disappointed and frustrated, but I haven’t really been keeping track of whether it’s nearly every day or most of the day. I guess I’d say… I haven’t noticed it as a constant sadness, no.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things—like your husband's health, finances, or other everyday matters—and have trouble controlling that worry, even when you're not actively trying to organize or finish tasks?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I’m not sure I’d call it excessive worry, exactly. I mean, I do think about those things—of course I do. At my age there are always appointments and bills and little household things, and I can get concerned when I’m staring at a paper and can’t make sense of what I’m supposed to do with it. But it doesn’t feel like I’m lying awake with my mind racing about ten different disasters, if that’s what you mean.

It’s more that I get tangled up when I’m trying to handle something, and then I feel frustrated or uneasy because I know I’m not getting it done. But when I’m not right in the middle of trying to sort things out, I don’t know that I’m worrying nonstop about everything. I haven’t really noticed that as the main problem.

I can still picture sitting there, everybody else already lining up or going out, and I’d be looking down at my paper like, “Oh… I didn’t finish that.”\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say this came out of nowhere recently. It’s more like it’s been part of me for a very long time, but now it’s causing more trouble.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you been feeling down, sad, or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve been sitting around crying all day, every day, or feeling hopeless in that heavy way you mean, I don’t think. I do get discouraged—very discouraged—when I look around and realize I still haven’t done the things I meant to do, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as being “down” most of the day.\n\nSome days I feel sort of worn out by myself, if that makes sense, like I’m disappointed and frustrated, but I haven’t really been keeping track of whether it’s nearly every day or most of the day. I guess I’d say… I haven’t noticed it as a constant sadness, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things—like your husband's health, finances, or other everyday matters—and have trouble controlling that worry, even when you're not actively trying to organize or finish tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it excessive worry, exactly. I mean, I do think about those things—of course I do. At my age there are always appointments and bills and little household things, and I can get concerned when I’m staring at a paper and can’t make sense of what I’m supposed to do with it. But it doesn’t feel like I’m lying awake with my mind racing about ten different disasters, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that I get tangled up when I’m trying to handle something, and then I feel frustrated or uneasy because I know I’m not getting it done. But when I’m not right in the middle of trying to sort things out, I don’t know that I’m worrying nonstop about everything. I haven’t really noticed that as the main problem.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo these kinds of organizing and follow-through difficulties show up in other areas of your life too—like when you're out with friends, handling other responsibilities, or doing things outside the kitchen table paperwork—or is it mostly limited to that one situation?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I had to give up leading a big senior-center event, and my daughter has started double-checking bills and appointments because she no longer trusts that I can keep everything straight on my own.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh no, it’s definitely not just the paperwork at the kitchen table. That’s where it’s most obvious, I suppose, because the papers are sitting there staring at me, but it spreads into other parts of my life too.

I actually had to give up leading this big event at the senior center, which was… honestly, that was hard for me. I’d done things like that before, and I wanted to be useful, you know? But there were too many moving pieces—who needed to be called, what had to be reserved, what time things were supposed to happen, little details people were counting on me to remember. I kept thinking, “I’ll make a list, I’ll get it straight,” and then I’d either misplace the list or not follow through on half of it, and I started feeling like I was going to let everyone down. Eventually I had to step back from it, which was embarrassing.

And at home, my daughter has started double-checking things now—bills, appointments, that sort of thing. She’s kind about it, mostly, but I can tell she doesn’t really trust me to keep it all straight on my own anymore. She’ll ask, “Did you pay this?” or “Are you sure the appointment is Tuesday and not Thursday?” And I hate that, because I’ve managed a household for years. I don’t like feeling like someone has to look over my shoulder.

So yes, it’s broader than one little situation. The paperwork is just the place where it piles up where everyone can see it.

I guess I’d say… I haven’t noticed it as a constant sadness, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things—like your husband's health, finances, or other everyday matters—and have trouble controlling that worry, even when you're not actively trying to organize or finish tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it excessive worry, exactly. I mean, I do think about those things—of course I do. At my age there are always appointments and bills and little household things, and I can get concerned when I’m staring at a paper and can’t make sense of what I’m supposed to do with it. But it doesn’t feel like I’m lying awake with my mind racing about ten different disasters, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that I get tangled up when I’m trying to handle something, and then I feel frustrated or uneasy because I know I’m not getting it done. But when I’m not right in the middle of trying to sort things out, I don’t know that I’m worrying nonstop about everything. I haven’t really noticed that as the main problem.\n\nDoctor: Do these kinds of organizing and follow-through difficulties show up in other areas of your life too—like when you're out with friends, handling other responsibilities, or doing things outside the kitchen table paperwork—or is it mostly limited to that one situation?\n\nPatient: Oh no, it’s definitely not just the paperwork at the kitchen table. That’s where it’s most obvious, I suppose, because the papers are sitting there staring at me, but it spreads into other parts of my life too.\n\nI actually had to give up leading this big event at the senior center, which was… honestly, that was hard for me. I’d done things like that before, and I wanted to be useful, you know? But there were too many moving pieces—who needed to be called, what had to be reserved, what time things were supposed to happen, little details people were counting on me to remember. I kept thinking, “I’ll make a list, I’ll get it straight,” and then I’d either misplace the list or not follow through on half of it, and I started feeling like I was going to let everyone down. Eventually I had to step back from it, which was embarrassing.\n\nAnd at home, my daughter has started double-checking things now—bills, appointments, that sort of thing. She’s kind about it, mostly, but I can tell she doesn’t really trust me to keep it all straight on my own anymore. She’ll ask, “Did you pay this?” or “Are you sure the appointment is Tuesday and not Thursday?” And I hate that, because I’ve managed a household for years. I don’t like feeling like someone has to look over my shoulder.\n\nSo yes, it’s broader than one little situation. The paperwork is just the place where it piles up where everyone can see it.\n\n[Last Question]\nAside from trouble organizing tasks, have you noticed any new problems with your memory—like forgetting recent conversations, misplacing things in unusual places, or getting confused about where you are or what day it is?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness\nmanifestation: \"I’ve missed returning calls, left bills sitting under a stack of mail, forgotten errands I promised my daughter I’d do, and more than once realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, not like I don’t know where I am or anything like that, but I have noticed more forgetting in ordinary day-to-day things. And it’s hard for me to separate what’s “memory” from what’s me just not keeping track, if that makes sense.

I’ll miss returning calls, for example. Someone will leave a message, and I’ll think, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then the whole day goes by. Or I’ll find a bill sitting under a stack of mail and realize it’s been there much longer than I thought. That’s happened more than I’d like to admit.

I’ve also forgotten errands I told my daughter I would do. Nothing dramatic, but enough that she’ll ask about it later and I’ll get that awful sinking feeling because I truly meant to do it. And with my husband’s appointments, more than once I realized too late that I never confirmed one—like I had the paper, or I meant to make the call, and somehow it just never got done.

So yes, I am forgetting things, mostly those everyday responsibilities and follow-ups. I don’t think I’m confused about the day or where I am, but I’m definitely dropping things I used to be able to hold onto better.

Someone will leave a message, and I’ll think, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then the whole day goes by. Or I’ll find a bill sitting under a stack of mail and realize it’s been there much longer than I thought. That’s happened more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI’ve also forgotten errands I told my daughter I would do. Nothing dramatic, but enough that she’ll ask about it later and I’ll get that awful sinking feeling because I truly meant to do it. And with my husband’s appointments, more than once I realized too late that I never confirmed one—like I had the paper, or I meant to make the call, and somehow it just never got done.\n\nSo yes, I am forgetting things, mostly those everyday responsibilities and follow-ups. I don’t think I’m confused about the day or where I am, but I’m definitely dropping things I used to be able to hold onto better.\n\n[Last Question]\nCompared to how you were functioning even a few years ago, would you say your memory and organizing ability have noticeably declined recently, or does this feel like the same lifelong pattern just becoming harder to compensate for as things pile up?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it’s completely new, no. That’s the part that makes me hesitate when you say “declined.” I mean, maybe it’s more visible now, but when I look back, there was always something like this.

Even when I was a little girl, my teachers used to write comments like, “bright but careless,” which I remember because my mother would get so frustrated—like, if I was bright, why wasn’t I just doing it properly? They’d say I forgot my books, forgot homework, and needed reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. So it wasn’t that I couldn’t understand things. It was more like I didn’t keep hold of all the steps unless someone was right there nudging me along.

So I suppose I’d say it feels like an old pattern, not something that appeared out of nowhere. But now it’s harder to cover for it. At school there was always a teacher saying, “Finish this first,” and now I’m supposed to be the one remembering everything and keeping myself on track, and that’s where it catches up with me.

since my husband started needing regular help with his appointments and daily arrangements.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S005\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do these kinds of organizing and follow-through difficulties show up in other areas of your life too—like when you're out with friends, handling other responsibilities, or doing things outside the kitchen table paperwork—or is it mostly limited to that one situation?\n\nPatient: Oh no, it’s definitely not just the paperwork at the kitchen table. That’s where it’s most obvious, I suppose, because the papers are sitting there staring at me, but it spreads into other parts of my life too.\n\nI actually had to give up leading this big event at the senior center, which was… honestly, that was hard for me. I’d done things like that before, and I wanted to be useful, you know? But there were too many moving pieces—who needed to be called, what had to be reserved, what time things were supposed to happen, little details people were counting on me to remember. I kept thinking, “I’ll make a list, I’ll get it straight,” and then I’d either misplace the list or not follow through on half of it, and I started feeling like I was going to let everyone down. Eventually I had to step back from it, which was embarrassing.\n\nAnd at home, my daughter has started double-checking things now—bills, appointments, that sort of thing. She’s kind about it, mostly, but I can tell she doesn’t really trust me to keep it all straight on my own anymore. She’ll ask, “Did you pay this?” or “Are you sure the appointment is Tuesday and not Thursday?” And I hate that, because I’ve managed a household for years. I don’t like feeling like someone has to look over my shoulder.\n\nSo yes, it’s broader than one little situation. The paperwork is just the place where it piles up where everyone can see it.\n\nDoctor: Aside from trouble organizing tasks, have you noticed any new problems with your memory—like forgetting recent conversations, misplacing things in unusual places, or getting confused about where you are or what day it is?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not like I don’t know where I am or anything like that, but I have noticed more forgetting in ordinary day-to-day things. And it’s hard for me to separate what’s “memory” from what’s me just not keeping track, if that makes sense.\n\nI’ll miss returning calls, for example. Someone will leave a message, and I’ll think, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then the whole day goes by. Or I’ll find a bill sitting under a stack of mail and realize it’s been there much longer than I thought. That’s happened more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI’ve also forgotten errands I told my daughter I would do. Nothing dramatic, but enough that she’ll ask about it later and I’ll get that awful sinking feeling because I truly meant to do it. And with my husband’s appointments, more than once I realized too late that I never confirmed one—like I had the paper, or I meant to make the call, and somehow it just never got done.\n\nSo yes, I am forgetting things, mostly those everyday responsibilities and follow-ups. I don’t think I’m confused about the day or where I am, but I’m definitely dropping things I used to be able to hold onto better.\n\nDoctor: Compared to how you were functioning even a few years ago, would you say your memory and organizing ability have noticeably declined recently, or does this feel like the same lifelong pattern just becoming harder to compensate for as things pile up?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s completely new, no. That’s the part that makes me hesitate when you say “declined.” I mean, maybe it’s more visible now, but when I look back, there was always something like this.\n\nEven when I was a little girl, my teachers used to write comments like, “bright but careless,” which I remember because my mother would get so frustrated—like, if I was bright, why wasn’t I just doing it properly? They’d say I forgot my books, forgot homework, and needed reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. So it wasn’t that I couldn’t understand things. It was more like I didn’t keep hold of all the steps unless someone was right there nudging me along.\n\nSo I suppose I’d say it feels like an old pattern, not something that appeared out of nowhere. But now it’s harder to cover for it. At school there was always a teacher saying, “Finish this first,” and now I’m supposed to be the one remembering everything and keeping myself on track, and that’s where it catches up with me.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any new difficulty with things like following the plot of a TV show or conversation, finding the right words when speaking, or getting confused or lost in places that used to be familiar to you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think so… not in that way. I mean, I may have to ask someone to repeat themselves if I wasn’t really paying attention, especially if there’s noise or if I’m trying to do two things at once, but I don’t feel like I’m losing the thread of conversations in some new or frightening way.

With TV shows, I can usually follow them if I’m actually sitting and watching. If I get up during it or start thinking about something else, then of course I’ll ask, “Wait, why is he there now?” but that feels more like I missed a piece, not that I can’t understand the story.

Words… I suppose everybody my age sometimes has a word on the tip of their tongue. I’ll say, “Oh, you know, the thing for the…,” and then it comes to me later. But I haven’t noticed that I’m suddenly unable to express myself or using the wrong words all the time.

And no, I’m not getting lost in familiar places. I know where I am. I know how to get to the grocery store, the senior center, my daughter’s house, places like that. So I wouldn’t say those are the problems, no. It’s more that if I’m not focused, things pass me by, but I’m not confused about where I am or what’s going on.

I’d forget to bring the right books home, or I’d leave homework somewhere, or I’d have it half done and not turned in. I do get discouraged—very discouraged—when I look around and realize I still haven’t done the things I meant to do, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as being “down” most of the day.\n\nSome days I feel sort of worn out by myself, if that makes sense, like I’m disappointed and frustrated, but I haven’t really been keeping track of whether it’s nearly every day or most of the day. I mean, it’s gotten much harder to manage now, but when I think back, there were signs even when I was a little girl.\n\nI remember my teachers would write things on my report cards like, “She’s bright, but careless,” which used to sting because I wasn’t trying to be careless. I’d forget to bring the right books home, or I’d leave homework somewhere, or I’d have it half done and not turned in. And they’d have to remind me to finish my seatwork before I could go outside to play. I can still picture sitting there, everybody else already lining up or going out, and I’d be looking down at my paper like, “Oh… I didn’t finish that.”\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say this came out of nowhere recently. It’s more like it’s been part of me for a very long time, but now it’s causing more trouble.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you been feeling down, sad, or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve been sitting around crying all day, every day, or feeling hopeless in that heavy way you mean, I don’t think. I do get discouraged—very discouraged—when I look around and realize I still haven’t done the things I meant to do, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as being “down” most of the day.\n\nSome days I feel sort of worn out by myself, if that makes sense, like I’m disappointed and frustrated, but I haven’t really been keeping track of whether it’s nearly every day or most of the day. I guess I’d say… I haven’t noticed it as a constant sadness, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things—like your husband's health, finances, or other everyday matters—and have trouble controlling that worry, even when you're not actively trying to organize or finish tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it excessive worry, exactly. I mean, I do think about those things—of course I do. At my age there are always appointments and bills and little household things, and I can get concerned when I’m staring at a paper and can’t make sense of what I’m supposed to do with it. But it doesn’t feel like I’m lying awake with my mind racing about ten different disasters, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that I get tangled up when I’m trying to handle something, and then I feel frustrated or uneasy because I know I’m not getting it done. But when I’m not right in the middle of trying to sort things out, I don’t know that I’m worrying nonstop about everything. I haven’t really noticed that as the main problem.\n\nDoctor: Do these kinds of organizing and follow-through difficulties show up in other areas of your life too—like when you're out with friends, handling other responsibilities, or doing things outside the kitchen table paperwork—or is it mostly limited to that one situation?\n\nPatient: Oh no, it’s definitely not just the paperwork at the kitchen table. That’s where it’s most obvious, I suppose, because the papers are sitting there staring at me, but it spreads into other parts of my life too.\n\nI actually had to give up leading this big event at the senior center, which was… honestly, that was hard for me. I’d done things like that before, and I wanted to be useful, you know? But there were too many moving pieces—who needed to be called, what had to be reserved, what time things were supposed to happen, little details people were counting on me to remember. I kept thinking, “I’ll make a list, I’ll get it straight,” and then I’d either misplace the list or not follow through on half of it, and I started feeling like I was going to let everyone down. Eventually I had to step back from it, which was embarrassing.\n\nAnd at home, my daughter has started double-checking things now—bills, appointments, that sort of thing. She’s kind about it, mostly, but I can tell she doesn’t really trust me to keep it all straight on my own anymore. She’ll ask, “Did you pay this?” or “Are you sure the appointment is Tuesday and not Thursday?” And I hate that, because I’ve managed a household for years. I don’t like feeling like someone has to look over my shoulder.\n\nSo yes, it’s broader than one little situation. The paperwork is just the place where it piles up where everyone can see it.\n\nDoctor: Aside from trouble organizing tasks, have you noticed any new problems with your memory—like forgetting recent conversations, misplacing things in unusual places, or getting confused about where you are or what day it is?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not like I don’t know where I am or anything like that, but I have noticed more forgetting in ordinary day-to-day things. And it’s hard for me to separate what’s “memory” from what’s me just not keeping track, if that makes sense.\n\nI’ll miss returning calls, for example. Someone will leave a message, and I’ll think, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then the whole day goes by. Or I’ll find a bill sitting under a stack of mail and realize it’s been there much longer than I thought. That’s happened more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI’ve also forgotten errands I told my daughter I would do. Nothing dramatic, but enough that she’ll ask about it later and I’ll get that awful sinking feeling because I truly meant to do it. And with my husband’s appointments, more than once I realized too late that I never confirmed one—like I had the paper, or I meant to make the call, and somehow it just never got done.\n\nSo yes, I am forgetting things, mostly those everyday responsibilities and follow-ups. I don’t think I’m confused about the day or where I am, but I’m definitely dropping things I used to be able to hold onto better.\n\nDoctor: Compared to how you were functioning even a few years ago, would you say your memory and organizing ability have noticeably declined recently, or does this feel like the same lifelong pattern just becoming harder to compensate for as things pile up?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s completely new, no. That’s the part that makes me hesitate when you say “declined.” I mean, maybe it’s more visible now, but when I look back, there was always something like this.\n\nEven when I was a little girl, my teachers used to write comments like, “bright but careless,” which I remember because my mother would get so frustrated—like, if I was bright, why wasn’t I just doing it properly? They’d say I forgot my books, forgot homework, and needed reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. So it wasn’t that I couldn’t understand things. It was more like I didn’t keep hold of all the steps unless someone was right there nudging me along.\n\nSo I suppose I’d say it feels like an old pattern, not something that appeared out of nowhere. But now it’s harder to cover for it. At school there was always a teacher saying, “Finish this first,” and now I’m supposed to be the one remembering everything and keeping myself on track, and that’s where it catches up with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any new difficulty with things like following the plot of a TV show or conversation, finding the right words when speaking, or getting confused or lost in places that used to be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not in that way. I mean, I may have to ask someone to repeat themselves if I wasn’t really paying attention, especially if there’s noise or if I’m trying to do two things at once, but I don’t feel like I’m losing the thread of conversations in some new or frightening way.\n\nWith TV shows, I can usually follow them if I’m actually sitting and watching. If I get up during it or start thinking about something else, then of course I’ll ask, “Wait, why is he there now?” but that feels more like I missed a piece, not that I can’t understand the story.\n\nWords… I suppose everybody my age sometimes has a word on the tip of their tongue. I’ll say, “Oh, you know, the thing for the…,” and then it comes to me later. But I haven’t noticed that I’m suddenly unable to express myself or using the wrong words all the time.\n\nAnd no, I’m not getting lost in familiar places. I know where I am. I know how to get to the grocery store, the senior center, my daughter’s house, places like that. So I wouldn’t say those are the problems, no. It’s more that if I’m not focused, things pass me by, but I’m not confused about where I am or what’s going on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Yes, I do notice that more than I’d like to admit. Not so much that I’m jumping up and running around, but if I’m supposed to sit still and be polite—like in a meeting or something at the senior center—I catch myself doing all these little things.

I’ll be rubbing my hands together, or tapping my foot under the chair, or clicking a pen without realizing it. Sometimes I shift around so much in the chair that I notice people glance over, and then I feel embarrassed and try to stop. But then a few minutes later I’m doing something else—picking at my sleeve, moving my feet, clasping and unclasping my hands.

It’s like my body doesn’t quite stay quiet even when I’m trying to. I can sit there, but it takes effort, and I’m often sort of fidgeting my way through it.

I guess I’d say… I haven’t noticed it as a constant sadness, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things—like your husband's health, finances, or other everyday matters—and have trouble controlling that worry, even when you're not actively trying to organize or finish tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it excessive worry, exactly. But it doesn’t feel like I’m lying awake with my mind racing about ten different disasters, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that I get tangled up when I’m trying to handle something, and then I feel frustrated or uneasy because I know I’m not getting it done. But when I’m not right in the middle of trying to sort things out, I don’t know that I’m worrying nonstop about everything. I haven’t really noticed that as the main problem.\n\nDoctor: Do these kinds of organizing and follow-through difficulties show up in other areas of your life too—like when you're out with friends, handling other responsibilities, or doing things outside the kitchen table paperwork—or is it mostly limited to that one situation?\n\nPatient: Oh no, it’s definitely not just the paperwork at the kitchen table. That’s where it’s most obvious, I suppose, because the papers are sitting there staring at me, but it spreads into other parts of my life too.\n\nI actually had to give up leading this big event at the senior center, which was… honestly, that was hard for me. I’d done things like that before, and I wanted to be useful, you know? But there were too many moving pieces—who needed to be called, what had to be reserved, what time things were supposed to happen, little details people were counting on me to remember. I kept thinking, “I’ll make a list, I’ll get it straight,” and then I’d either misplace the list or not follow through on half of it, and I started feeling like I was going to let everyone down. Eventually I had to step back from it, which was embarrassing.\n\nAnd at home, my daughter has started double-checking things now—bills, appointments, that sort of thing. She’s kind about it, mostly, but I can tell she doesn’t really trust me to keep it all straight on my own anymore. She’ll ask, “Did you pay this?” or “Are you sure the appointment is Tuesday and not Thursday?” And I hate that, because I’ve managed a household for years. I don’t like feeling like someone has to look over my shoulder.\n\nSo yes, it’s broader than one little situation. The paperwork is just the place where it piles up where everyone can see it.\n\nDoctor: Aside from trouble organizing tasks, have you noticed any new problems with your memory—like forgetting recent conversations, misplacing things in unusual places, or getting confused about where you are or what day it is?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not like I don’t know where I am or anything like that, but I have noticed more forgetting in ordinary day-to-day things. And it’s hard for me to separate what’s “memory” from what’s me just not keeping track, if that makes sense.\n\nI’ll miss returning calls, for example. Someone will leave a message, and I’ll think, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then the whole day goes by. Or I’ll find a bill sitting under a stack of mail and realize it’s been there much longer than I thought. That’s happened more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI’ve also forgotten errands I told my daughter I would do. Nothing dramatic, but enough that she’ll ask about it later and I’ll get that awful sinking feeling because I truly meant to do it. And with my husband’s appointments, more than once I realized too late that I never confirmed one—like I had the paper, or I meant to make the call, and somehow it just never got done.\n\nSo yes, I am forgetting things, mostly those everyday responsibilities and follow-ups. I don’t think I’m confused about the day or where I am, but I’m definitely dropping things I used to be able to hold onto better.\n\nDoctor: Compared to how you were functioning even a few years ago, would you say your memory and organizing ability have noticeably declined recently, or does this feel like the same lifelong pattern just becoming harder to compensate for as things pile up?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s completely new, no. That’s the part that makes me hesitate when you say “declined.” I mean, maybe it’s more visible now, but when I look back, there was always something like this.\n\nEven when I was a little girl, my teachers used to write comments like, “bright but careless,” which I remember because my mother would get so frustrated—like, if I was bright, why wasn’t I just doing it properly? They’d say I forgot my books, forgot homework, and needed reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. So it wasn’t that I couldn’t understand things. It was more like I didn’t keep hold of all the steps unless someone was right there nudging me along.\n\nSo I suppose I’d say it feels like an old pattern, not something that appeared out of nowhere. But now it’s harder to cover for it. At school there was always a teacher saying, “Finish this first,” and now I’m supposed to be the one remembering everything and keeping myself on track, and that’s where it catches up with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any new difficulty with things like following the plot of a TV show or conversation, finding the right words when speaking, or getting confused or lost in places that used to be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not in that way. I mean, I may have to ask someone to repeat themselves if I wasn’t really paying attention, especially if there’s noise or if I’m trying to do two things at once, but I don’t feel like I’m losing the thread of conversations in some new or frightening way.\n\nWith TV shows, I can usually follow them if I’m actually sitting and watching. If I get up during it or start thinking about something else, then of course I’ll ask, “Wait, why is he there now?” but that feels more like I missed a piece, not that I can’t understand the story.\n\nWords… I suppose everybody my age sometimes has a word on the tip of their tongue. I’ll say, “Oh, you know, the thing for the…,” and then it comes to me later. But I haven’t noticed that I’m suddenly unable to express myself or using the wrong words all the time.\n\nAnd no, I’m not getting lost in familiar places. I know where I am. I know how to get to the grocery store, the senior center, my daughter’s house, places like that. So I wouldn’t say those are the problems, no. It’s more that if I’m not focused, things pass me by, but I’m not confused about where I am or what’s going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, have trouble sitting still for long periods, or find yourself interrupting others or acting impulsively without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do notice that more than I’d like to admit. Not so much that I’m jumping up and running around, but if I’m supposed to sit still and be polite—like in a meeting or something at the senior center—I catch myself doing all these little things.\n\nI’ll be rubbing my hands together, or tapping my foot under the chair, or clicking a pen without realizing it. Sometimes I shift around so much in the chair that I notice people glance over, and then I feel embarrassed and try to stop. But then a few minutes later I’m doing something else—picking at my sleeve, moving my feet, clasping and unclasping my hands.\n\nIt’s like my body doesn’t quite stay quiet even when I’m trying to. I can sit there, but it takes effort, and I’m often sort of fidgeting my way through it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging). Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNo, it’s not completely new. I mean, it’s gotten much harder to manage now, but when I think back, there were signs even when I was a little girl.\n\nI remember my teachers would write things on my report cards like, “She’s bright, but careless,” which used to sting because I wasn’t trying to be careless. I’d forget to bring the right books home, or I’d leave homework somewhere, or I’d have it half done and not turned in. And they’d have to remind me to finish my seatwork before I could go outside to play. I can still picture sitting there, everybody else already lining up or going out, and I’d be looking down at my paper like, “Oh… I didn’t finish that.”\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say this came out of nowhere recently. It’s more like it’s been part of me for a very long time, but now it’s causing more trouble.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation. Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. She’s kind about it, mostly, but I can tell she doesn’t really trust me to keep it all straight on my own anymore. She’ll ask, “Did you pay this?” or “Are you sure the appointment is Tuesday and not Thursday?” And I hate that, because I’ve managed a household for years. I don’t like feeling like someone has to look over my shoulder.\n\nSo yes, it’s broader than one little situation. Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.
